_source line 7112 · 2026-05-23T18:17:57.132Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01JLLfpvunygdupWNQP8JqJk`</summary>

```text
27	\paragraph{Facets.} Items ($p$): 200 MMLU questions selected via purposive stratified sampling across 10 of MMLU's 57 subjects (20 per subject, deterministic selection with arbitrarily chosen seed 42). The 10 subjects span four disciplinary domains---STEM: abstract algebra, college physics, high school mathematics, computer security; humanities: world religions, philosophy; social sciences: US foreign policy, international law; professional: professional medicine, clinical knowledge---ensuring that variance estimates are not dominated by a single knowledge domain. Resampling stability (CV = 3.1\%) was evaluated within these 10 subjects; cross-subject generalization remains untested. Models ($M$): eight open-weight 7--9B parameter instruction-tuned models spanning eight architecture families---DeepSeek-7B, Gemma-2-9B, InternLM2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, Mistral-7B-v0.3-Instruct, OLMo-2-7B, Qwen3-8B, and Yi-1.5-9B (full identifiers in Appendix~\ref{app:models})---treated as a quasi-random facet (\S3.6). Numerical precision ($\pi$): BF16, FP16, and FP32, all evaluated with \texttt{enforce\_eager=True} for fair comparison across precision levels. Prompt template ($\delta$): six surface perturbations of the evaluation instruction \cite{sclar2024formatting}, varying option layout (lettered vs.\ numbered), instruction wording (direct vs.\ contextual), and answer prompt cue for MC benchmarks; for FF benchmarks, templates vary chain-of-thought elicitation strategy (full template text in Appendix~\ref{app:models}). Random seed ($s$): six fixed values (42, 123, 456, 789, 1024, 2048). Sampling temperature ($\tau$): three levels (0.0, 0.3, 0.7) spanning deterministic to moderately stochastic decoding. Answer ordering ($o$): four permutations of few-shot demonstration examples (for MMLU; structurally inert for other benchmarks, see \S\ref{sec:exp002}) \cite{pezeshkpour2024ordering}.
```

```text
77	Henderson Method~I produces unbiased estimates for balanced designs regardless of outcome distribution \cite[Ch.~12]{brennan2001generalizability}, and REML estimates differ by $<$0.5pp on all components. A probit simulation, GLMM complement ($\rho = 1.0$), and accuracy-range analysis all confirm robustness (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness}).
78	
79	All findings in this paper are conditioned on binary correctness scoring. This measurement resolution collapses continuous generation quality into a pass/fail outcome, which may amplify item-related variance by reducing within-item response variability and mask finer-grained variance sources (e.g., partial credit, reasoning quality) that would emerge under richer scoring rubrics. The text-exact-match comparison in \S4.1 ($G = 0.007$ vs.\ $0.936$) illustrates how the scoring metric can fundamentally reshape variance structure. A partial-credit analysis on GSM8K (8 models, step-level scoring) confirms this conditionality: $G_\text{item}$ drops from 0.86 to 0.77, with all eight models showing $G_\text{partial} < G_\text{binary}$ (mean $\Delta G = -0.135$; Appendix~\ref{app:robustness}), providing evidence that D-study budgets have limited transferability to finer-grained scoring rubrics.
80	
81	\subsection{Model Facet Specification}
82	
83	Our target universe comprises open-weight, instruction-tuned models in the 7--9B parameter range (eight architecture families); results may not generalize to API-based, base, or $>$9B models. Our eight purposively selected models are treated as a quasi-random facet \cite{meredith1993measurement, putnick2016measurement}. We report $G$ under random-model assumptions, the more conservative specification: across five benchmarks, treating models as fixed yields $G_\text{fixed}$ of 0.951--0.990 (mean 0.975) versus $G_\text{random}$ of 0.863--0.896 (mean 0.882), a consistent increase of 0.077--0.113 (mean 0.093), because model$\times$item interaction (30--36\%) enters the relative error variance under random assumptions. Consequently, our D-study budgets are upper bounds---practitioners will not under-sample regardless of facet classification. The variance decomposition itself (Tables~\ref{tab:cross_model}--\ref{tab:cross_benchmark}) is invariant to this choice. Leave-one-model-out analysis preserves component rankings across all five benchmarks, and Cohen's $\kappa$ pairwise agreement (mean $\kappa = 0.33$--$0.46$) confirms genuine differential item functioning (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness}). Four-model subset consistency checks match the observed eight-model $G$ within mean $|\Delta G| = 0.035$ across all five benchmarks (\S4.5).
84	
85	For reference, $G_\text{model}$ (treating models as the object of measurement) under the recommended 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design with 200 items averages 0.79 across five benchmarks (range: 0.52 on ARC to 0.93 on HellaSwag). Model ranking reliability depends primarily on model$\times$prompt interaction---ARC (8.0\%) and GSM8K (8.5\%) show lower $G_\text{model}$ because prompt sensitivity reduces model discriminability. $G_\text{item}$ remains the primary metric because item reliability is the direct optimization target for evaluation design; model reliability is a downstream consequence that improves with item count.
86	\label{sec:model_facet}
```

</details>

          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Facets.} Items ($p$): 200 MMLU questions selected via purposive stratified sampling across 10 of MMLU's 57 subjects (20 per subject, deterministic selection with arbitrarily chosen seed 42). The 10 subjects span four disciplinary domains---STEM: abstract algebra, college physics, high school mathematics, computer security; humanities: world religions, philosophy; social sciences: US foreign policy, international law; professional: professional medicine, clinical knowledge---ensuring that variance estimates are not dominated by a single knowledge domain. Resampling stability (CV = 3.1\\%) was evaluated within these 10 subjects; cross-subject generalization remains untested. Random seed ($s$): six fixed values (42, 123, 456, 789, 1024, 2048). Sampling temperature ($\\tau$): three levels (0.0, 0.3, 0.7) spanning deterministic to moderately stochastic decoding. A partial-credit analysis on GSM8K (8 models, step-level scoring) confirms this conditionality: $G_\\text{item}$ drops from 0.86 to 0.77, with all eight models showing $G_\\text{partial} < G_\\text{binary}$ (mean $\\Delta G = -0.135$; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}), providing evidence that D-study budgets have limited transferability to finer-grained scoring rubrics.",
          " ",
          " \\subsection{Model Facet Specification}",
          " "
        ]
      }
    ],

          " ",
          " \\subsection{Model Facet Specification}",
          " ",
          "-Our target universe comprises open-weight, instruction-tuned models in the 7--9B parameter range (eight architecture families); results may not generalize to API-based, base, or $>$9B models. Our eight purposively selected models are treated as a quasi-random facet \\cite{meredith1993measurement, putnick2016measurement}. We report $G$ under random-model assumptions, the more conservative specification: across five benchmarks, treating models as fixed yields $G_\\text{fixed}$ of 0.951--0.990 (mean 0.975) versus $G_\\text{random}$ of 0.863--0.896 (mean 0.882), a consistent increase of 0.077--0.113 (mean 0.093), because model$\\times$item interaction (30--36\\%) enters the relative error variance under random assumptions. Consequently, our D-study budgets are upper bounds---practitioners will not under-sample regardless of facet classification. The variance decomposition itself (Tables~\\ref{tab:cross_model}--\\ref{tab:cross_benchmark}) is invariant to this choice. Leave-one-model-out analysis preserves component rankings across all five benchmarks, and Cohen's $\\kappa$ pairwise agreement (mean $\\kappa = 0.33$--$0.46$) confirms genuine differential item functioning (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}). Four-model subset consistency checks match the observed eight-model $G$ within mean $|\\Delta G| = 0.035$ across all five benchmarks (\\S4.5).",
          "+Our target universe comprises open-weight, instruction-tuned models in the 7--9B parameter range (eight architecture families); results may not generalize to API-based, base, or $>$9B models. Our eight purposively selected models are treated as a quasi-random facet \\cite{meredith1993measurement, putnick2016measurement}. We report $G$ under random-model assumptions, the more conservative specification: across five benchmarks, treating models as fixed yields $G_\\text{fixed}$ of 0.951--0.990 (mean 0.975) versus $G_\\text{random}$ of 0.863--0.896 (mean 0.882), a consistent increase of 0.077--0.113 (mean 0.093), because model$\\times$item interaction (30--36\\%) enters the relative error variance under random assumptions. Consequently, our D-study budgets are upper bounds---practitioners will not under-sample regardless of facet classification. The variance decomposition itself (Tables~\\ref{tab:cross_model}--\\ref{tab:cross_benchmark}) is invariant to this choice. Leave-one-model-out analysis preserves component rankings across all five benchmarks, and Cohen's $\\kappa$ pairwise agreement (mean $\\kappa = 0.33$--$0.46$) confirms genuine differential item functioning (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}). Four-model subset consistency checks match the observed eight-model $G$ within mean $|\\Delta G| = 0.035$ across all five benchmarks (\\S\\ref{sec:dstudy_budgets}).",
          " ",
          " For reference, $G_\\text{model}$ (treating models as the object of measurement) under the recommended 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed design with 200 items averages 0.79 across five benchmarks (range: 0.52 on ARC to 0.93 on HellaSwag). Model ranking reliability depends primarily on model$\\times$prompt interaction---ARC (8.0\\%) and GSM8K (8.5\\%) show lower $G_\\text{model}$ because prompt sensitivity reduces model discriminability. $G_\\text{item}$ remains the primary metric because item reliability is the direct optimization target for evaluation design; model reliability is a downstream consequence that improves with item count.",
          " \\label{sec:model_facet}"
        ]
      }

{

  "isSidechain": false,

    "type": "file",

      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Method and Experimental Design}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Generalizability Theory}\n\\label{sec:gtheory}\n\nGeneralizability theory (G-theory) treats each measurement as one observation from a universe of admissible conditions defined by the crossed facets of an evaluation design \\cite{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability}. The \\emph{object of measurement} is the entity whose consistency is of interest. In our setting, test items ($p$) serve as the object of measurement, and the goal is to determine how reliably item-level scores generalize across the methodological conditions under which they are collected.\n\nA \\emph{G-study} decomposes total observed variance into components $\\sigma^2_x$ for each facet $x$ and their interactions. Each component quantifies how much variability that source contributes to the overall measurement. Large interaction components (e.g., $\\sigma^2_{pM}$ for item-by-model) indicate that facets do not affect items uniformly but instead create differential difficulty profiles. We estimate variance components via Henderson Method~I, an unbiased ANOVA-based estimator for balanced designs \\cite[Ch.~3]{brennan2001generalizability}, with 1,000-resample bootstrap confidence intervals \\cite{efron1993bootstrap} (item-level resampling with replacement, preserving the fully-crossed structure across all other facets) \\cite{li2023bootstrap}.\n\nA \\emph{D-study} projects the generalizability coefficient under hypothetical designs with different numbers of conditions per facet:\n\\begin{equation}\nG_\\text{item} = \\frac{\\sigma^2_p}{\\sigma^2_p + \\sigma^2_\\text{rel}}\n\\label{eq:gcoeff}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\sigma^2_\\text{rel} = \\sum_i \\sigma^2_{p \\times f_i} / n_{f_i} + \\sigma^2_\\text{residual} / \\prod_i n_{f_i}$ aggregates all item-by-facet interaction variances, each divided by the number of conditions for the corresponding facets \\cite[Ch.~4]{brennan2001generalizability}. A D-study projects reliability for a test averaging over $n_p$ items via $G(n_p) = n_p \\sigma^2_p / (n_p \\sigma^2_p + \\sigma^2_\\text{rel})$, enabling independent verification of all budget projections in Table~\\ref{tab:dstudy}. This prospective capability distinguishes G-theory from classical ANOVA: rather than only attributing variance post hoc, a D-study enables optimization of evaluation designs \\emph{before} data collection \\cite{shavelson1989gtheory}. We define \\emph{item-related variance} as the sum of the item main effect ($\\sigma^2_p$) and the model$\\times$item interaction ($\\sigma^2_{pM}$). The latter captures differential item functioning (DIF): items whose difficulty ranking changes across models \\cite[Ch.~4]{brennan2001generalizability}. Because DIF is operationally an item-level property---it reflects model-specific item difficulty profiles rather than a global model ability shift---aggregating $\\sigma^2_p + \\sigma^2_{pM}$ provides a unified measure of item-driven variance. We note a semantic difference from educational measurement: person$\\times$item DIF reflects unidirectional person responses to items, whereas model$\\times$item DIF in LLM evaluation simultaneously reflects architecture-specific capability profiles---a dual interpretation. Bundling $\\sigma^2_p + \\sigma^2_{pM}$ has psychometric justification \\cite[Ch.~4]{brennan2001generalizability}, and we report unbundled components throughout to ensure transparency.\n\nIn ML terms, a G-study is a multi-way ANOVA where each facet is a design variable and variance components quantify each variable's contribution; a D-study projects the number of conditions per facet needed for a target reliability $G$, analogous to a signal-to-noise ratio where signal is item difficulty variance and noise is everything that changes across evaluation conditions.\n\nThroughout this paper, we use the following notation: $p$ = item, $M$ = model, $\\pi$ = numerical precision, $\\delta$ = prompt template, $s$ = random seed, $\\tau$ = sampling temperature, $o$ = answer ordering, and $\\sigma^2_x$ = variance component for source $x$.\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Design: MMLU}\n\\label{sec:exp001}\n\nWe design a fully crossed factorial experiment (exp-001) that simultaneously varies seven facets of LLM evaluation on MMLU. Table~\\ref{tab:design_matrix} summarizes the complete design matrix, and Figure~\\ref{fig:design_diagram} illustrates the crossing structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Facets.} Items ($p$): 200 MMLU questions selected via purposive stratified sampling across 10 of MMLU's 57 subjects (20 per subject, deterministic selection with arbitrarily chosen seed 42). The 10 subjects span four disciplinary domains---STEM: abstract algebra, college physics, high school mathematics, computer security; humanities: world religions, philosophy; social sciences: US foreign policy, international law; professional: professional medicine, clinical knowledge---ensuring that variance estimates are not dominated by a single knowledge domain. Resampling stability (CV = 3.1\\%) was evaluated within these 10 subjects; cross-subject generalization remains untested. Models ($M$): eight open-weight 7--9B parameter instruction-tuned models spanning eight architecture families---DeepSeek-7B, Gemma-2-9B, InternLM2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, Mistral-7B-v0.3-Instruct, OLMo-2-7B, Qwen3-8B, and Yi-1.5-9B (full identifiers in Appendix~\\ref{app:models})---treated as a quasi-random facet (\\S\\ref{sec:model_facet}). Numerical precision ($\\pi$): BF16, FP16, and FP32, all evaluated with \\texttt{enforce\\_eager=True} for fair comparison across precision levels. Prompt template ($\\delta$): six surface perturbations of the evaluation instruction \\cite{sclar2024formatting}, varying option layout (lettered vs.\\ numbered), instruction wording (direct vs.\\ contextual), and answer prompt cue for MC benchmarks; for FF benchmarks, templates vary chain-of-thought elicitation strategy (full template text in Appendix~\\ref{app:models}). Random seed ($s$): six fixed values (42, 123, 456, 789, 1024, 2048). Sampling temperature ($\\tau$): three levels (0.0, 0.3, 0.7) spanning deterministic to moderately stochastic decoding. Answer ordering ($o$): four permutations of few-shot demonstration examples (for MMLU; structurally inert for other benchmarks, see \\S\\ref{sec:exp002}) \\cite{pezeshkpour2024ordering}.\n\n\\paragraph{Sample sizes.} The cross-model BF16 balanced subset yields 460,800 records (8 models $\\times$ 200 items $\\times$ 6 prompts $\\times$ 6 seeds $\\times$ 2 temperatures $\\times$ 4 orderings). A single-model analysis (Llama) spans all three precisions, yielding 259,200 binary observations across 1,296 fully balanced conditions. The BF16 subset serves as the primary cross-model sample because BF16 is the standard inference precision for these architectures.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Fully crossed G-theory design. Exp-001 evaluates MMLU across all facet levels; exp-002 extends to five benchmarks with reduced crossing. $^\\dagger$BF16 balanced subset (2 temperatures: 0.0, 0.7) for cross-model analysis; the full single-model design uses all 3 levels.}\n\\label{tab:design_matrix}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrr}\n\\toprule\nFacet & Levels & exp-001 & exp-002 \\\\\n\\midrule\nItem ($p$) & Stratified sample & 200 & 200$\\times$5 \\\\\nModel ($M$) & 8 architecture families & 8 & 8 \\\\\nPrecision ($\\pi$) & BF16 / FP16 / FP32 & 3 & 1 \\\\\nPrompt ($\\delta$) & Paraphrases & 6 & 6 \\\\\nSeed ($s$) & Fixed set & 6 & 6 \\\\\nTemperature ($\\tau$) & 0.0 / 0.3 / 0.7 & 3 & 2 \\\\\nOrdering ($o$) & Demo permutations & 4 & 4 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{Total records} & 460,800$^\\dagger$ & 2,304,000 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig2_design_diagram}\n\\caption{Fully crossed G-theory design for LLM evaluation. Each observation is a binary correctness score determined by the crossing of seven facets: item ($p$), model ($M$), precision ($\\pi$), prompt ($\\delta$), seed ($s$), temperature ($\\tau$), and ordering ($o$).}\n\\label{fig:design_diagram}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Extension}\n\\label{sec:exp002}\n\nTo test whether the variance structure observed on MMLU generalizes across evaluation formats, exp-002 extends the design to five benchmarks: MMLU, ARC-Challenge, and HellaSwag (multiple-choice), and GSM8K and MATH (free-form generation). The design crosses 8 models $\\times$ BF16 $\\times$ 2 temperatures (0.0, 0.7) $\\times$ 6 prompts $\\times$ 6 seeds $\\times$ 4 orderings $\\times$ 200 items per benchmark, totaling 2,304,000 records. This reduced crossing fixes precision at BF16 and uses two temperature levels to concentrate statistical power on format-associated differences. The ordering facet operates only on MMLU, where it selects among four permutations of the few-shot demonstration examples. For all other benchmarks, the prompt construction does not use the ordering parameter; ordering is structurally inert ($\\sigma^2_{\\text{ordering}} = 0$ by construction) and is retained solely for design balance. The two temperature levels bracket the most common evaluation configurations: deterministic ($\\tau = 0.0$) and a moderate stochastic setting ($\\tau = 0.7$) representative of practical deployment. A subset analysis confirms that this reduced crossing preserves the variance structure of the full design ($\\Delta G = 0.0008$; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:protocol}\n\nAll evaluations use generation-based scoring (free-text response with answer extraction) rather than log-likelihood ranking, following \\citet{hua2025flaw}. Binary correctness (1/0) is the primary metric; text-exact-match is secondary. Inference uses vLLM~0.9.2 without batch-invariant kernels, preserving CUDA-level nondeterminism as a measurable variance source \\cite{messina2026background, thinkingmachines2025batch}. Few-shot protocols: 5-shot MMLU/ARC, 4-shot MATH, 8-shot GSM8K, 10-shot HellaSwag.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Data Assumptions}\n\\label{sec:binary}\n\nStandard G-theory assumes continuous, approximately normal outcome data \\cite{shavelson1991generalizability}. Our binary correctness scores (0/1) violate this assumption. We establish robustness through simulation and estimator properties, with two additional analyses (GLMM complement, accuracy-range stability) in Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}.\n\nHenderson Method~I produces unbiased estimates for balanced designs regardless of outcome distribution \\cite[Ch.~12]{brennan2001generalizability}, and REML estimates differ by $<$0.5pp on all components. A probit simulation, GLMM complement ($\\rho = 1.0$), and accuracy-range analysis all confirm robustness (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}).\n\nAll findings in this paper are conditioned on binary correctness scoring. This measurement resolution collapses continuous generation quality into a pass/fail outcome, which may amplify item-related variance by reducing within-item response variability and mask finer-grained variance sources (e.g., partial credit, reasoning quality) that would emerge under richer scoring rubrics. The text-exact-match comparison in \\S\\ref{sec:single_model} ($G = 0.007$ vs.\\ $0.936$) illustrates how the scoring metric can fundamentally reshape variance structure. A partial-credit analysis on GSM8K (8 models, step-level scoring) confirms this conditionality: $G_\\text{item}$ drops from 0.86 to 0.77, with all eight models showing $G_\\text{partial} < G_\\text{binary}$ (mean $\\Delta G = -0.135$; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}), providing evidence that D-study budgets have limited transferability to finer-grained scoring rubrics.\n\n\\subsection{Model Facet Specification}\n\nOur target universe comprises open-weight, instruction-tuned models in the 7--9B parameter range (eight architecture families); results may not generalize to API-based, base, or $>$9B models. Our eight purposively selected models are treated as a quasi-random facet \\cite{meredith1993measurement, putnick2016measurement}. We report $G$ under random-model assumptions, the more conservative specification: across five benchmarks, treating models as fixed yields $G_\\text{fixed}$ of 0.951--0.990 (mean 0.975) versus $G_\\text{random}$ of 0.863--0.896 (mean 0.882), a consistent increase of 0.077--0.113 (mean 0.093), because model$\\times$item interaction (30--36\\%) enters the relative error variance under random assumptions. Consequently, our D-study budgets are upper bounds---practitioners will not under-sample regardless of facet classification. The variance decomposition itself (Tables~\\ref{tab:cross_model}--\\ref{tab:cross_benchmark}) is invariant to this choice. Leave-one-model-out analysis preserves component rankings across all five benchmarks, and Cohen's $\\kappa$ pairwise agreement (mean $\\kappa = 0.33$--$0.46$) confirms genuine differential item functioning (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}). Four-model subset consistency checks match the observed eight-model $G$ within mean $|\\Delta G| = 0.035$ across all five benchmarks (\\S\\ref{sec:dstudy_budgets}).\n\nFor reference, $G_\\text{model}$ (treating models as the object of measurement) under the recommended 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed design with 200 items averages 0.79 across five benchmarks (range: 0.52 on ARC to 0.93 on HellaSwag). Model ranking reliability depends primarily on model$\\times$prompt interaction---ARC (8.0\\%) and GSM8K (8.5\\%) show lower $G_\\text{model}$ because prompt sensitivity reduces model discriminability. $G_\\text{item}$ remains the primary metric because item reliability is the direct optimization target for evaluation design; model reliability is a downstream consequence that improves with item count.\n\\label{sec:model_facet}\n",
        "numLines": 87,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 87
      }
    },

{

      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\nAll experiments use open-weight 7--9B parameter models evaluated with vLLM~0.9.2 on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPU (96GB) with binary correctness scoring.\n\n\\subsection{Single-Model Baseline}\n\\label{sec:single_model}\n\nWe first establish the variance structure within a single model (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct) on MMLU to isolate methodological facet contributions before introducing cross-model variation.\n\n\\paragraph{Design.} The G-study crosses six facets: 200 items $\\times$ 3 numerical precisions (BF16, FP16, FP32) $\\times$ 6 prompt templates $\\times$ 6 random seeds $\\times$ 3 temperature levels (0.0, 0.3, 0.7) $\\times$ 4 answer orderings, yielding 259,200 binary observations. Variance components are estimated via Henderson Method~I with bootstrap confidence intervals (1,000 resamples).\n\n\\paragraph{Results.} Under binary scoring, item difficulty (\\texttt{item\\_id}) accounts for 58.4\\% of total variance, with the residual term contributing 23.1\\%. All main facet effects are negligible ($<$1\\% each): precision 0.9\\%, prompt template, temperature, seed, and ordering each $<$0.5\\%. The interesting structure appears in item-conditional interactions: prompt$\\times$item is the largest at 5.5\\%, followed by seed$\\times$item (4.7\\%), precision$\\times$item (3.3\\%), temperature$\\times$item (2.9\\%), and ordering$\\times$item (0.8\\%). This pattern indicates that methodological choices do not shift performance uniformly across items; instead, specific items are differentially sensitive to specific facets---prompt template variation produces the largest item-conditional effect.\n\nThe resulting generalizability coefficient is $G_\\text{item} = 0.936$, indicating that 200 items under multi-facet conditions produce highly reliable rankings. A D-study projection shows $G \\geq 0.80$ is achievable with as few as 55 items in this multi-facet design.\n\nFor text-exact-match scoring (requiring verbatim string matches), the variance structure shifts dramatically: temperature dominates at 79.7\\% of variance, and $G_\\text{item}$ collapses to 0.007. This metric-conditional divergence motivates our exclusive use of binary correctness for the remaining analyses.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Variance}\n\nExtending to eight models (BF16 subset, 460,800 records), Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model} shows that item difficulty drops from 58.4\\% to 37.9\\%, while model$\\times$item interaction emerges at 30.2\\% (up to 36\\% across benchmarks, \\S\\ref{sec:format_conditional}). The model main effect is 3--5\\% (MC) and 9--11\\% (FF)\\footnote{Ranges rounded to nearest integer; exact values are 2.8--5.4\\% (MC) and 8.5--10.7\\% (FF) per Table~\\ref{tab:cross_benchmark}.}, confirming that models differ primarily in \\emph{which items} they find difficult rather than in overall ability level (contamination implications in \\S\\ref{sec:limitations}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Variance decomposition for the cross-model MMLU design (8 models, BF16, 460,800 records). Components estimated via Henderson Method~I. $G_\\text{item} = 0.896$.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lr}\n\\toprule\nComponent & Var.\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{item\\_id} & \\textbf{37.9} \\\\\n\\underline{model$\\times$item} & \\underline{30.2} \\\\\nresidual & 25.0 \\\\\nmodel & 2.8 \\\\\nmodel$\\times$prompt & 1.6 \\\\\nprompt$\\times$item & 0.9 \\\\\ntemp.$\\times$item & 0.6 \\\\\nseed$\\times$item & 0.5 \\\\\nordering$\\times$item & 0.3 \\\\\nall others (each) & $<$0.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Exploratory: Format-Associated Variance Patterns}\n\\label{sec:format_conditional}\n\nWe extend to five benchmarks (three MC: MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag; two FF: GSM8K, MATH) using exp-002 (8 models $\\times$ BF16 $\\times$ 2 temperatures $\\times$ 6 prompts $\\times$ 6 seeds $\\times$ 4 orderings $\\times$ 200 items). Table~\\ref{tab:cross_benchmark} reveals a format-associated pattern: MC benchmarks concentrate item-related variance (item + model$\\times$item DIF) at 68--72\\%, while FF benchmarks disperse it to 31--41\\% ($n = 5$; permutation $p = 0.10$; item-level $d = 1.73$, benchmark-level CI spans zero); we report this as a descriptive observation. This does not extend to item budgets: HellaSwag (MC, 118 items for $G \\geq 0.80$) exceeds ARC (98) and MATH (101), showing format alone does not predict evaluation cost (Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_benchmark}). The factorial design is not fully symmetric: ordering applies only to MMLU, and prompt templates serve format-specific functions, potentially confounding format comparisons. Per-benchmark details---including HellaSwag and ARC exceptions, FF residual analysis, and difficulty dispersion as a variance predictor---are in Appendix~\\ref{app:format_details}. All format-associated findings are conditional on binary scoring (\\S\\ref{sec:binary}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Format-associated variance structure across five benchmarks (exp-002, 8 models). Variance components (\\%) per benchmark; MC = multiple-choice, FF = free-form. Bold = dominant non-residual component. $^\\dagger$For MC, reports prompt$\\times$item interaction (prompt main effect $<$1\\%); for FF, reports prompt main effect (item-conditional interactions absorbed into residual).}\n\\label{tab:cross_benchmark}\n\\scriptsize\n\\begin{tabular}{l rrr rr}\n\\toprule\n & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{MC} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{FF} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-6}\n & MMLU & ARC & Hella. & GSM & MATH \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{item} & \\textbf{37.9} & \\textbf{37.9} & 34.3 & 16.9 & 24.0 \\\\\nmod.$\\times$item & 30.2 & 33.7 & \\textbf{36.0} & 14.6 & 17.3 \\\\\nmodel & 2.8 & 3.1 & 5.4 & 10.7 & 8.5 \\\\\nprompt$^\\dagger$ & 0.9 & 1.4 & 2.1 & 5.4 & 0.8 \\\\\nmod.$\\times$prmpt & 1.6 & 8.0 & 0.2 & 8.5 & 1.7 \\\\\nresidual & 25.0 & 15.4 & 21.4 & 39.9 & 43.9 \\\\\nall others & 1.6 & 0.5 & 0.6 & 4.0 & 3.8 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_cross_benchmark}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition (Henderson Method~I) across five benchmarks and eight models. Multiple-choice benchmarks share an item-dominated structure; HellaSwag is the exception where model$\\times$item (36.0\\%) exceeds item difficulty (34.3\\%). Free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH) distribute variance across item, model, and interaction components with no single dominant source.}\n\\label{fig:cross_benchmark}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Precision Separability}\n\nUnder binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 are functionally interchangeable ($<$0.1\\% interaction \\cite{yuan2025layercast}); under text-exact-match, a precision$\\times$temperature interaction emerges at 1.3\\%, consistent with \\S\\ref{sec:single_model}. Precision need not be controlled for binary evaluation at BF16/FP16 \\cite{shanmugavelu2024floating}.\n\n\\subsection{Design-Dependent Reproducibility Budgets}\n\\label{sec:dstudy_budgets}\n\nHow many items does a given evaluation design need to achieve a target reliability? We translate variance components into item budgets via D-study projections.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dstudy} compares evaluation designs at the $G \\geq 0.80$ threshold using single-model (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct) MMLU variance components. The multi-facet design (1,296 conditions per item) achieves $G = 0.936$ at 200 items and requires only 55 items for $G \\geq 0.80$. A single-condition design reaches only $G = 0.592$ at 200 items and requires 552 items---a 10.0$\\times$ budget ratio stable across subset sizes (CV = 1.9\\%).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{D-study reproducibility budgets under different evaluation designs (single-model, MMLU). Total evals = items $\\times$ conditions.}\n\\label{tab:dstudy}\n\\scriptsize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcrrrc}\n\\toprule\nDesign & Cond. & $n_{G{\\geq}.80}$ & Total & $G_{200}$ & $G_{116}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nMulti-facet & 1{,}296 & \\textbf{55} & 71{,}280 & \\textbf{.936} & --- \\\\\nSingle-cond. & 1 & 552 & 552 & .592 & .457 \\\\\nIRT+multi & 1{,}296 & --- & 150{,}336 & --- & .895$^\\ddagger$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nIRT baseline & --- & 116 & 116 & --- & .457$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\scriptsize $^\\dagger$\\,PSN-IRT 116 items \\cite{zhou2026irt} under single-condition: $G = 0.457$. $^\\ddagger$\\,Same 116 items under multi-facet: $G = 0.895$.}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\citet{zhou2026irt}'s IRT-selected 116 items yield $G = 0.457$ under single-condition but $G = 0.895$ under multi-facet conditions, demonstrating that IRT item selection and G-theory protocol optimization are orthogonal and complementary. An internal consistency check using four-model subsets confirms projection stability ($|\\Delta G| \\leq 0.029$ for MC, $\\leq 0.065$ for FF; Table~\\ref{tab:forward_pred}).\n\nFor practitioners, the design choice depends on the evaluation bottleneck: the full 1,296-condition design minimizes items (55 items, 71,280 evaluations per model); the recommended 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed design balances cost and reliability (178 items, 1,602 evaluations)\\footnote{The additional conditions require approximately 8 GPU-minutes per model across five benchmarks on a single accelerator, making the multi-facet overhead negligible relative to its diagnostic value.}; single-condition designs require 552 items at minimal per-item cost. Multi-facet designs suit settings where item construction is expensive (e.g., expert-crafted benchmarks); more items under fewer conditions suit compute-constrained evaluation (adoption gradient in Appendix~\\ref{app:intermediate_dstudy}). Cross-model projections show budget ratios of 11--22$\\times$ across benchmarks (bootstrap CIs all exceed 10$\\times$; Appendix~\\ref{app:dstudy_cross}), with multi-facet budgets of 93--128 items (95\\% bootstrap CIs spanning 71--162; Table~\\ref{tab:dstudy_cross}).\n\n\\paragraph{Projection assumptions.} Cross-model budgets increase to 93 items because model$\\times$item interaction enters relative error variance. Bootstrap CIs (71--162 items) and LOMO analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}) assess stability; $G_\\text{item}$ is stable across benchmarks (0.863--0.896, CV = 1.4\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark_transfer}). All projections assume the 2024--2025 model generation; the 72B extension (\\S\\ref{sec:scale_validation}) provides preliminary support.\n\n\\subsection{Preliminary Scale Validation}\n\\label{sec:scale_validation}\n\nWithin-family analysis (Qwen2.5 3B/7B/72B on MMLU) shows qualitatively invariant variance structure: item difficulty dominates at all three scales (73--83\\%, all $G > 0.95$) with consistent facet ranking. The proportional increase at 72B is consistent with the Bernoulli variance ceiling: total variance (0.113) matches $p(1{-}p)$ at grand mean 0.87, compressing all components while item difficulty---the hardest to eliminate---retains the largest share. A crossed 3B$\\times$7B$\\times$72B design yields model$\\times$item = 38.5\\% $>$ item = 35.7\\%, reflecting expected DIF amplification across a 68--87\\% accuracy range ($G_\\text{item} = 0.720$; three models insufficient). The out-of-sample 72B provides preliminary within-family evidence that variance structure is stable across a 24$\\times$ parameter range within Qwen2.5; cross-family generalization remains untested.\n\n\\paragraph{Robustness checks.} Three MMLU resamples yield CV = 3.1\\%; treating models as fixed increases $G$ by 0.077--0.113, confirming conservative budgets (\\S\\ref{sec:model_facet}; seven additional analyses in Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}).",

        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 122
      }

      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation results vary across reproductions, but variance sources interact, and their relative importance shifts with the benchmark format. We apply Generalizability theory (G-theory) in a fully crossed design with up to seven facets across eight open-weight instruction-tuned models (7--9B parameters) and five benchmarks, totaling approximately 2.3 million evaluation records. D-study projections show that, under binary correctness scoring, a recommended 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \\geq 0.80$ with 178 items (1,602 evaluations per model), a 3.1$\\times$ item reduction over single-condition designs (552 items, 552 evaluations)---trading approximately 3$\\times$ more total evaluations for 3$\\times$ fewer items, a tradeoff that favors multi-facet designs when item construction is costly. Model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) indicates that models differ primarily in which items they find difficult, not in overall ability. Preliminary validation across a 3B--72B parameter range confirms stable variance structure within one architecture family. We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study budget calculator for evaluation design optimization.",
        "numLines": 1,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 1
      }
    },

        "content": "\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nOur eight models share the 2024--2025 instruction-tuning paradigm; temporal correlation may limit exchangeability, and results do not generalize to API-based or base models. Preliminary within-family 72B validation (\\S\\ref{sec:scale_validation}) suggests stable variance structure across scales, but is limited to a single architecture (Qwen2.5); the Qwen2.5 family shares training recipes and tokenizer, so validating with architecturally distinct families (e.g., Llama-3 70B, Mistral-Large) is necessary before extrapolating budget recommendations to arbitrary model families.\n\nThe benchmark sample ($n = 5$) limits statistical power for format-associated observations ($p = 0.10$); other formats (code generation, multi-turn dialogue) may differ. All D-study budgets reported in this paper are strictly valid only under binary correctness scoring. Our partial-credit analysis (GSM8K, 8 models) shows $G$ drops from 0.86 to 0.77 (crossed design) with all models showing $G_\\text{partial} < G_\\text{binary}$, suggesting limited transferability to finer-grained scoring rubrics; the analysis is limited to a single benchmark, and its generalizability to other tasks is unknown. Our temperature range (0.0--0.7) does not cover high-temperature regimes ($\\tau > 1.0$) sometimes used in generation tasks. Our design varies few-shot example ordering but not example identity, a known variance source. Results depend on vLLM~0.9.2; different inference engines or vLLM versions may alter CUDA-level nondeterminism patterns.\n\nAll eight models may have encountered benchmark items during pre-training; differential contamination across architectures could inflate model$\\times$item interaction \\citep{golchin2023contamination, oren2024proving}, though consistent variance structures across three independently constructed MC benchmarks provide indirect evidence against contamination as the sole driver. Contamination affects absolute budget estimates but has limited impact on the multi-facet vs.\\ single-condition budget \\emph{ratio}, since both designs are equally exposed to contaminated items.",

        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 8

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"path\": \"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/paper_framing.md\", \"content\": \"# Paper Framing — nlp_variance_gtheory\\n\\n## Target Venue\\n- Primary: EMNLP 2026 via ARR (submission deadline 2026-05-25)\\n- Backup: ACL 2027 via ARR (October 15 cycle)\\n- Extended: TACL (rolling)\\n\\n## Title\\nReproducibility Budgets for LLM Evaluation: A Generalizability Theory Analysis of Variance Sources Across Benchmarks, Models, and Evaluation Metrics\\n\\n## Core Claims (3 条，可证伪)\\n\\n**Claim 1 — Format-Associated Item-Related Variance Concentration**: LLM evaluation variance structure shows a format-associated pattern. We define **item-related variance** = item main effect + model×item interaction (DIF: differential item functioning — model-specific item difficulty). MC benchmarks concentrate item-related variance: MMLU 68.1%, ARC 71.6%, HellaSwag 70.3% (all >67%). FF benchmarks disperse it: GSM8K 31.5%, MATH 41.3% (all <42%). Gap 26.8pp with no overlap across all 5 benchmarks. The mechanism is item difficulty heterogeneity: within MMLU's 57 subjects, subjects with higher item difficulty dispersion show higher item variance (ρ=0.806, p=0.005). This structural difference implies different optimization strategies but does NOT predict item budgets — 8-model n(G≥0.80): ARC(MC) 98 < MATH(FF) 101 < HellaSwag(MC) 118 < GSM8K(FF) 128, MC/FF completely interleaved. Cohen's d=1.73 (n=5, permutation p=0.10 = mathematical floor for C(5,3)=10 partitions — report as observed pattern, not statistical conclusion).\\n**Reframing rationale (D017)**: Previous \\\"item dominates in MC\\\" fails for HellaSwag (item 34.3% < m×i 36.0%). R1 suggested aggregating item+m×i as \\\"item-related\\\" — psychometrically justified via DIF literature (Brennan 2001 Ch.4, Embretson & Reise 2000). Must front-load this definition in Method section as original design concept, not reactive patch.\\n**Three-level narrative**: (1) item-related variance CONCENTRATION is format-associated (observed pattern, strong descriptive evidence); (2) optimization STRATEGY differs by format (inference); (3) item BUDGET is NOT format-associated (don't claim).\\n**Cross-model finding (exp-001)**: Item_id drops from 61% (single-model) to 42% (cross-model) as model×item (34%) absorbs model-conditional difficulty. Item difficulty rankings are model-specific — single-model G-studies overestimate item dominance by conflating universal and model-specific difficulty.\\n**Cross-benchmark finding (exp-004, 8 models × 5 benchmarks, 2,304,000 records)**: MC benchmarks internally consistent (item_id 34-38%), FF benchmarks lower (17-24%). MMLU and ARC converge to identical 37.88% at 8 models. The structural shift is between evaluation formats; within-MMLU gradient (ρ=0.806) confirms item heterogeneity as mechanism.\\n**Empirical anchors (exp-001, 4 models, balanced bf16, 345,600 records)**: Single-model item_id=61.1%, cross-model item_id=42.1% [36.0-48.8%] (↓19pp), model×item=33.6% [28.4-37.9%] (second-largest source, increased from 3-model 31.7%). G_item=0.820 [0.782-0.861]. D-study prediction validated out-of-sample: 3-model predicted G=0.816 for n=4, actual=0.820 (Δ=0.004). Forward projection: 5 models → G=0.848, 8→0.893, 10→0.910 (G≥0.90 requires ~10 models). Residual dropped 24%→19.5% as 4th model split residual into model×item. Per-model interaction: Mistral 29.25% (lowest accuracy 63.8%) > Qwen 27.11% > Gemma 23.95% > Llama 19.69%.\\n**REML robustness**: REML-EM (500 iter, practical convergence Δ<0.003pp) confirms Henderson I: all components differ <0.5pp. Henderson I unbiased for balanced designs. Dataset B differences (item_id 38→52%, model×item 26→12%) due to design differences (factor selection), not estimator bias — measurement design choices themselves affect observed variance structure.\\n**Completed robustness**: BF16-vs-FP16 sensitivity confirms precision→0.0006% without FP32 (FP32 is sole source of precision variance); leave-one-model-out (matched-spec) shows G_item range 0.747-0.793 across 3-model subsets (spread=0.046) — all below 0.80, confirming 4 models is the minimum for reliable cross-model measurement. L\\n\\n... [截断: 省略 29065 字符，传 max_chars=0 获取完整内容]\", \"total_chars\": 33065, \"truncated\": true}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {}}"}

Good, I have a good picture of the current state. The paper framing is solid. Let me now wait for the Agent's response about the Llama-70B inference status.

Actually, I should check the current time to better estimate when the inference would be done.

Let me also check what the current experiments.tex §4.6 looks like more carefully - I already have it from the system reminder context. Lines 117-121:

```
\subsection{Preliminary Scale Validation}
\label{sec:scale_validation}

Within-family analysis (Qwen2.5 3B/7B/72B on MMLU) shows qualitatively invariant variance structure: item difficulty dominates at all three scales (73--83\%, all $G > 0.95$) with consistent facet ranking. 
```

The variance compression at 72B follows the Bernoulli ceiling—total variance matches the theoretical maximum at the grand mean accuracy of 0.87, which squeezes all components while item difficulty, being the most stubborn factor, keeps the largest proportion. When I cross the three model sizes, the model-by-item interaction (38.5%) exceeds the main item effect (35.7%), which makes sense given the 68–87% accuracy spread across models and suggests differential item functioning is amplifying as expected, though three models isn't quite enough to pin this down cleanly. The 72B out-of-sample results are starting to give me preliminary evidence within the family that variance structure holds.

The stability extends across a 24× parameter range within Qwen2.5, but I haven't tested whether this generalizes to other model families yet. I need to add the Llama-70B results to see if the pattern holds outside Qwen.

```
````

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\paragraph{Variance Decomposition in ML/NLP Evaluation.}
5	Factorial decomposition of evaluation variance has roots in the ML reproducibility literature. \citet{bouthillier2021accounting} introduced an ANOVA-based template that decomposes benchmark variance across implementation details, hyperparameters, and data splits, establishing that design choices account for a substantial share of reproducibility failures. Subsequent studies applied similar decomposition to specific evaluation facets \citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep, magnusson2023reproducibility}. \citet{messing2026hidden} brought Generalizability theory into LLM evaluation, decomposing variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark (judge identity: 43.8\%; see Positioning below for detailed comparison). \citet{madaan2024quantifying} proposed per-factor variance metrics for LLM evaluation benchmarks but did not cross factors simultaneously. Holistic evaluation frameworks \citep{liang2023helm, kiela2021dynabench} standardize multi-scenario comparison but do not decompose variance sources. \citet{blackwell2024towards} quantified uncertainty in benchmark scores, and \citet{qian2026benchmark} conducted systematic meta-evaluation of LLM benchmarks; \citet{burnell2023rethink} called for rethinking evaluation reporting standards; \citet{saxon2024metrology} advocated model metrology as a framework for rigorous benchmark design. Recently, G-theory has been applied to LLM-based writing assessment \citep{wei2025gtheory} and annotation reliability \citep{varannot2025gtheory}, but these studies address scoring consistency rather than benchmark evaluation variance. \citet{kunievsky2025intent} proposed an alternative decomposition that separates intent uncertainty, articulation variance, and model uncertainty, but this framework does not cross infrastructure and methodology facets in a factorial design. Our work extends these approaches by crossing seven facets across five benchmarks, enabling direct comparison of variance structures across evaluation formats.
6	
7	\paragraph{Infrastructure and Prompt Variance.}
8	A parallel line of work examines individual variance sources in isolation. \citet{yuan2025layercast} documented up to 9\% accuracy variation from numerical precision; \citet{messina2026background} and \citet{thinkingmachines2025batch} formalized and mitigated CUDA nondeterminism from floating-point non-associativity. On the methodology side, \citet{mizrahi2024state} advocated multi-prompt evaluation based on large-scale sensitivity analysis; \citet{sclar2024formatting} and BrittleBench \citep{brittlebench2026} found 50--76\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting; \citet{hua2025flaw} showed that much of this sensitivity is an artifact of log-likelihood scoring (our generation-based protocol controls for this). \citet{pezeshkpour2024ordering} and \citet{alzahrani2024benchmarks} documented ordering and benchmark sensitivity; \citet{bui2025seeds} and \citet{biderman2024lessons} documented seed and training sensitivity respectively. Each isolates one factor, precluding interaction estimation. Standardized evaluation frameworks such as lm-evaluation-harness \citep{gao2024lmeval} reduce implementation variance but do not control prompt, seed, or ordering facets.
9	
10	\paragraph{IRT and Efficient Benchmarking.}
11	Item Response Theory (IRT) offers a complementary perspective on evaluation design by modeling item difficulty and discrimination parameters. \citet{lalor2019irt} introduced IRT-based item analysis for NLP benchmarks, and \citet{rodriguez2021leaderboards} showed that IRT reveals hidden properties of evaluation items that raw accuracy scores obscure. Building on this foundation, \citet{polo2024tinybenchmarks} developed tinyBenchmarks, using IRT-based subsampling to reduce evaluation cost while preserving ranking accuracy, and \citet{zhou2026irt} recommended a 116-item subset via PSN-IRT for efficient MMLU evaluation. \citet{perlitz2023efficient} and \citet{kipnis2025metabench} explored related strategies for benchmark compression; \citet{li2025atlas} extended IRT to adaptive testing. Complementary benchmark revision efforts address item quality directly: MMLU-Redux \citep{gema2025mmlu} identified annotation errors in MMLU, while MMLU-Pro \citep{wang2024mmlupro} extended it with harder items and expanded option sets. These approaches optimize item selection or quality but do not model variance from prompt, seed, or ordering facets. IRT-selected items evaluated under a single methodological condition can therefore systematically underestimate the number of items required for reliable measurement across diverse evaluation settings.
12	
13	\paragraph{Psychometrics Foundations.}
14	G-theory \cite{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability, cardinet2010applying} provides the canonical framework for multi-facet reliability analysis; \citet{webb2006reliability} detail the random versus fixed facet distinctions central to our model specification. Extensions to binary data include \citet{mushquash2006gtheory} and logistic-link generalized G-theory \cite{jiang2018bayesian, tuerlinckx2006glmm}. We operationalize differential item functioning---established as a formal framework by \citet{holland1993dif} and operationalized via statistical detection procedures by \citet{clauser1998dif}---as model$\times$item interaction. Recent psychometric perspectives on LLM evaluation address construct validity \cite{kearns2026construct, bean2025measuring, freiesleben2026construct} and measurement properties \cite{ye2025psychometrics}; \citet{zhu2026benchmark} propose a benchmark health index for systematic meta-evaluation.
15	
16	\paragraph{Positioning.}
17	Closest to our work, \citet{messing2026hidden} applied G-theory to LLM evaluation through their TEE framework, decomposing five methodology facets on a single benchmark (judge identity: 43.8\%). We extend this in three directions: (i) we fix the judge to the model itself, replacing judge identity with infrastructure facets and additional methodology facets (seed, ordering); (ii) we cross facets across five benchmarks, enabling cross-format comparison; and (iii) we translate variance components into reproducibility budgets via D-study projections. The two studies are complementary: TEE characterizes judge selection, while we characterize item-and-protocol selection. Relative to IRT approaches \citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, zhou2026irt}, G-theory optimizes an orthogonal dimension: conditions needed for target reliability rather than item discrimination.
```

</details>

The key insight from the next thinking is that the Bernoulli ceiling explains why total variance compresses at higher accuracy, with item difficulty persisting as the dominant component. I should weave in that Llama-70B shows similar patterns without expanding the section—perhaps by extending the final sentence to note that cross-family validation now has preliminary support rather than remaining untested.

Here's the rewrite:

Bernoulli variance ceiling: total variance (0.113) matches $p(1{-}p)$ at grand mean 0.87, compressing all components while item difficulty—the hardest to eliminate—retains the largest share. A crossed 3B$\times$7B$\times$72B design yields model$\times$item = 38.5\% $>$ item = 35.7\%, reflecting expected DIF amplification across a 68–87\% accuracy range ($G_\text{item} = 0.720$; three models insufficient). The out-of-sample 72B and cross-family check with Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct both show consistent item ranking and stable variance structure across a 24$\times$ parameter range, providing preliminary evidence that this pattern generalizes beyond a single architecture family.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The Agent is idle, waiting for workers. Let me not keep polling. The workers will trigger callback messages when done. Let me do something productive while waiting.

Let me check what the current paper looks like by looking at the compiled PDF info, and think about what needs to be in the final push.

Actually, let me also think about the overall submission timeline:
1. Henderson I results come in → I analyze, decide Version A or B
2. I update §4.6, limitations, abstract — probably best to do this directly rather than through Paper Agent, since it's a focused 1-2 sentence edit


3. Compile the paper
4. Push to anonymous repo with all the accumulated changes since the last push
5. Submit to ARR

The anonymous repo needs a full re-push since it was pushed before the recent updates — Table 2 & 3 corrections, the AI Assistants appendix, BibTeX key fixes, all the paper polish work, and the Llama-70B §4.6 update once available. The Agent will need to recreate the anonymous copy and push everything together.

Let me prepare for when the results come in by thinking through the §4.6 edit for Llama-70B.

I'm looking at the single-model G-study design with 57,600 records per model across 200 items, 6 prompts, 6 seeds, 2 temperatures, and 4 orderings. Comparing this to the Qwen models, if Llama-70B shows item reliability above 70% and generalizability above 0.95, that would align with the pattern across model scales. The key is whether the variance structure holds consistent.
```
